3128 AE5E C03A  30 	mov *r10+,r0
3129 AE60 0450  20 	b *r0
3130               	;
3131               	; 	' THE FONT'S COLOUR TABLE WAS 472 BYTES OF ONE REPEATED VALUE, which is a
3132               	; 	' fifth of what the fixed area had left. DEFINE COLOR copies such a table
3133               	; 	' out of ROM into all three screen thirds; writing the constant straight
3134               	; 	' into the colour table does the same job for the price of a loop, and
3135               	; 	' the loop runs once, at boot.
3136               	; 	'
3137               	; 	' The colour table is at >2000 and the VDP mirrors it once per screen
3138               	; 	' third, >800 apart. Both numbers were read out of the GENERATED assembly
3139               	; 	' -- define_color's `ai r0,>2000` and LDIRVM3's `ai r0,>0800` -- rather
3140               	; 	' than assumed, because a wrong base here would paint over the pattern
3141               	; 	' table and the failure would look like corrupt artwork.
3142               	; 	'
3143               	; 	' Paced: a few hundred VDP writes in one frame are silently dropped.
3144               	; 	' THE SCANNER'S COLOURS ARE THREE BLOCKS REPEATED SIXTEEN TIMES EACH, so
3145               	; 	' the table shipped 384 bytes to say 24 bytes' worth. Same trick as the
3146               	; 	' font: write the colour table directly. >2000 is the colour table's base
3147               	; 	' and >800 the stride between screen thirds -- the same layout DEFINE COLOR
3148               	; 	' walks for us in setup_font, which this cannot use because it is cheaper to
3149               	; 	' expand 24 bytes here than to ship 384 in the bank.
3150               	; 	' NOTHING IS UNDER THE GROUND FLOOR, SO NOTHING SHOULD BE GREEN THERE.
3151               	; 	' A floor bar is five pixels of yellow over three of the green air
3152               	; 	' belonging to the floor BELOW it (see SLAB), which is right for three of
3153               	; 	' the four bars and wrong for the last one: below floor 0 there is no
3154               	; 	' floor, only the scanner, and the green read as a strip of shop with
3155               	; 	' nothing in it.
3156               	; 	'
3157               	; 	' NO NEW CHARACTER IS NEEDED, BECAUSE THE COLOUR TABLE HAS THREE COPIES
3158               	; 	' -- one per eight screen rows -- and the bands land such that each bar
3159               	; 	' sits in a different third:
3160               	; 	'
3161               	; 	'     band 0 roof   rows  1-5    bar row  5   third 0
3162               	; 	'     band 1        rows  6-10   bar row 10   third 1
3163               	; 	'     band 2        rows 11-15   bar row 15   third 1
3164               	; 	'     band 3 GROUND rows 16-20   bar row 20   third 2
3165               	; 	'
3166               	; 	' so recolouring SLAB in third 2 alone reaches the ground floor's bar and
3167               	; 	' nothing else. The same third also holds the scanner, but the scanner
3168               	; 	' does not use these characters. This is the same mechanism that once hid
3169               	; 	' a bug for months (CLAUDE.md 3A, the blanked-in-one-third note) used
3170               	; 	' deliberately for once.
3171               	; 	'
3172               	; 	' Only the three bottom lines change, and only their BACKGROUND: SLAB has
3173               	; 	' no ink there and SLABE's brick keeps its grey.
3174               	; floor0_colour:
3175               cvb_FLOOR0_COLOUR
3176               	; 	#f0a = 12288			' colour table, bottom screen third
3177 AE62 0200  20 	li r0,12288
     AE64 3000

3596               	;
3597               	; 	' ------------------------------------------- ALPHA LOCK, CALIBRATED
3598               	; 	' On the TI, ALPHA LOCK shares a line with the joystick's VERTICAL axis.
3599               	; 	' Latched down it reports a direction that is NEVER RELEASED. Every other
3600               	; 	' game in this repo dodges this by not reading up/down at all; this one
3601               	; 	' cannot, because down is the duck and up is the elevator.
3602               	; 	'
3603               	; 	' THE FIRST VERSION OF THIS REFUSED TO START UNTIL THE AXIS CLEARED, AND
3604               	; 	' THAT WAS THE WRONG CALL. Classic99 defaults to invertcaps=1, so the TI
3605               	; 	' sees ALPHA LOCK DOWN when the host's Caps Lock is UP -- the normal
3606               	; 	' state. The game then sat on its title screen for ever waiting for a
3607               	; 	' condition the player had no reason to suspect, which presents as "the
3608               	; 	' title comes up and it will not start". A check that turns a survivable
3609               	; 	' input quirk into a dead game is worse than no check.
3610               	; 	'
3611               	; 	' So: sample the axis for 40 frames BEFORE any input can reasonably have
3612               	; 	' been given. A direction held for essentially all of them is not a player
3613               	; 	' -- it is the key. Record it, say so, and then IGNORE that direction for
3614               	; 	' the whole game, which makes ALPHA LOCK harmless instead of fatal.
3615               	; alock_cal:
3616               cvb_ALOCK_CAL

3828               	; 	' FIRE ONLY, AND THAT IS A DECISION RATHER THAN AN OVERSIGHT.
3829               	; 	'
3830               	; 	' `1` used to start the game as well, because on the TI joystick fire is
3831               	; 	' TAB -- neither guessable nor forgiving, since Windows treats a stray TAB
3832               	; 	' as a focus change and moves the whole window away. CLAUDE.md 3A still
3833               	; 	' says not to make FIRE the only way out of a title screen, and this screen
3834               	; 	' is the named exception.
3835               	; 	'
3836               	; 	' WHAT IT BUYS IS THE 838 SEQUENCE. Every digit on this screen now means
3837               	; 	' exactly one thing: a step of the cheat code, or a reset of it. While `1`
3838               	; 	' also meant START, mistyping one digit of 8-3-8 did not merely reset the
3839               	; 	' sequence -- it began a game, which is a far worse outcome than having to
3840               	; 	' type the code again, and it is the single most likely typo since 1 is
3841               	; 	' next to nothing on the sequence but adjacent to everything on a keypad.
3842               	; 	'
3843               	; 	' The hazard the digit guarded against is real but recoverable: a TAB that
3844               	; 	' moves focus leaves the title on screen and the player clicks back. A
3845               	; 	' game started by accident cannot be undone at all.

3864               	; 	' 8 cannot advance past the first step. It is an accident of the sequence
3865               	; 	' rather than a design, and it had two costs: 8,5,3,9,8 opened this page as
3866               	; 	' readily as 8,3,8, and a key that READS as held for many frames got a free
3867               	; 	' walk through the state machine.
3868               	; 	'
3869               	; 	' That second one is not hypothetical on this machine. ALPHA LOCK shorts a
3870               	; 	' keyboard line -- and Classic99 defaults to invertcaps, so it reads DOWN
3871               	; 	' with the host's Caps Lock UP -- and this page was reached twice from
3872               	; 	' single keypresses while testing an unrelated change.
3873               	; 	'

3875               	; 	' Bust-A-Bobble resets only on a stray 3 and still lets 8,5,3,8 through.

     B75C 0100

     B760 21B4     
4231               	; 					' first floor's traffic comes at him from

     B772 208C     
4242               	; 	kanim = 0
4243 B774 04C0  14 	clr r0
4244 B776 D800  38 	movb r0,@cvb_KANIM
     B778 20F8     
4245               	;
4246               	; 	' HARRY STARTS ON FLOOR 2 AT THE WEST EDGE OF SCREEN 7, and that is as
4247               	; 	' far left as the clock allows. The research says "the second-floor
4248               	; 	' elevator door", which is screen 3, and it cannot be done:
4249               	; 	'
4250               	; 	'   spawn      escape     round
4251               	; 	'   screen 3   119.9 s    100 s   -- impossible
4252               	; 	'   screen 5   108.1 s    100 s   -- impossible
4253               	; 	'   screen 6   102.3 s    100 s   -- impossible
4254               	; 	'   screen 7    96.4 s    100 s   -- 3.6 s of room
4255               	; 	'
4256               	; 	' HE STARTS AT THE LIFT NOW, AND THAT COST HIM A SPEED CHANGE.
4257               	; 	'
4258               	; 	' His escape route ZIGZAGS -- east along floor 2 to its escalator, WEST
4259               	; 	' along floor 3 to that one, then east again along the roof to the door.
4260               	; 	' From the lift on screen 3 that is 5,120 px against the 4,208 he walked
4261               	; 	' from screen 7, so at his old 1.75 px a pass he needed 105 s of a 100 s
4262               	; 	' round: he could never escape at all, and one of the two loss conditions
4263               	; 	' would quietly have stopped existing. This block used to say exactly that
4264               	; 	' and conclude the lift was impossible. It was impossible AT THAT SPEED.
4265               	; 	'
4266               	; 	' AND QUARTER-PIXELS COULD NOT EXPRESS THE ANSWER. A quarter of a pixel
4267               	; 	' per pass is worth about TEN SECONDS of his route, so the two candidate
4268               	; 	' values straddled the buzzer with nothing in between:
4269               	; 	'
4270               	; 	'   2.00 px/pass   escapes at 102.4 s of a 100 s round   never escapes
4271               	; 	'   2.25 px/pass   escapes at  91.0 s                    nine seconds early
4272               	; 	'
4273               	; 	' Worse, 2.25 was not reachable at all: the accumulator below had two
4274               	; 	' drain steps and therefore a hard ceiling of 2 px a pass, so hsp4 = 9
4275               	; 	' silently delivered 2.00 and he fell two thirds of a screen short of his
4276               	; 	' own escape. See the accumulator comment for the full account.
4277               	; 	'
4278               	; 	' Sixteenths with three drains put the step at about 2.5 s instead of 10,
4279               	; 	' and hsp64 = 35 (2.1875 px/pass) lands him at 96.3 s of the 100 --
4280               	; 	' escaping as the timer expires, which is what was asked for.
4281               	; 	'

4463               	; 	' HARRY'S SPEED IS IN QUARTER PIXELS, AND IT DOES NOT RAMP. It used to be
4464               	; 	' 2 px/frame against Kelly's 3, which sounds like a comfortable 1.5x --
4465               	; 	' and Kelly still lost the race to the roof by ELEVEN SECONDS, every
4466               	; 	' round, because the two routes are not the same length. Kelly runs
4467               	; 	' 7,992 px (three traverses plus the roof); Harry runs 4,104, because he
4468               	; 	' spawns beside floor 2's escalator and so skips a traverse. A chase
4469               	; 	' resolves on PATH / SPEED, and Kelly needs to be more than TWICE
4470               	; 	' Harry's speed before he is ahead at all. See DESIGN.md 4a.
4471               	; 	'
4472               	; 	' 1.75, AND THE NUMBER IS MEASURED, NOT GUESSED. Tracked off the
4473               	; 	' reference video (DESIGN.md 4a), the 2600's Kelly covers 0.40 screen
4474               	; 	' widths a second and its Harry 0.193 -- a ratio of 2.07. At 1.5 ours
4475               	; 	' was 2.67 and the crook visibly strolled. 1.75 brings it to 2.29.
4476               	; 	'
4477               	; 	' 2.0 would match the original exactly and still cannot be used, because
4478               	; 	' OUR routes are not the original's: Kelly's is 1.95x Harry's here, so a
4479               	; 	' speed ratio of 2.0 leaves him 5.2 s ahead at the escape edge -- less
4480               	; 	' than one beach ball (9 s). At 1.75 he is 10.1 s ahead, which absorbs a
4481               	; 	' hit. That figure counts the LIFT, which is on his way and carries him
4482               	; 	' two floors; the foot-only route is 5 s worse and is not the route
4483               	; 	' anybody takes.
4484               	; 	'
4485               	; 	' THE PER-KROOK DIAL IS STILL NOT HIS SPEED. It is the obstacles, which
4486               	; 	' is also the only dial the manual names (`obsp` above, and the ball
4487               	; 	' arcs). assets/checkchase.py checks all of this mechanically -- run it
4488               	; 	' if you touch these numbers.

4515 B906 0450  20 	b *r0
4516               	;
4517               	; 	' ======================================================================
4518               	; 	' DRAWING THE STORE -- four blits, once per screen crossing
4519               	; 	' ======================================================================
4520               	; 	' This is the whole cost of the flip, and it is paid on a discrete event
4521               	; 	' rather than every frame. RallyX's pan repaints 576 chars every time the
4522               	; 	' camera moves one cell; this repaints 640 about once every two seconds
4523               	; 	' of running.
4524               	; 	' SPRITES OFF BEFORE THE BLIT, NOT AFTER IT. This routine WAITs between
4525               	; 	' bands -- one band a frame, because a burst of VDP writes past a few
4526               	; 	' dozen in one frame is silently dropped -- so painting the new screen
4527               	; 	' takes several frames, and for all of them the OLD screen's actors and
4528               	; 	' obstacles were still sitting on top of it. Following Harry through a
4529               	; 	' seam showed him and every cart from the floor he had just left,
4530               	; 	' standing on a shop that was assembling itself underneath them.
4531               	; 	'
4532               	; 	' They are put back by the normal draw on the same pass, so the crossing
4533               	; 	' reads as a cut rather than as a dissolve with ghosts in it.
4534               	; 	' EACH BAND IS FINISHED BEFORE THE FRAME IT IS DRAWN IN ENDS, and the
4535               	; 	' bands go TOP DOWN. Both halves of that matter.
4536               	; 	'
4537               	; 	' The blit paints the template -- shelves, pillars and all -- and the
4538               	; 	' radios and prizes then take out the fixtures they stand in. Those two
4539               	; 	' used to be four frames apart: all four bands blitted with a WAIT
4540               	; 	' between them, and only afterwards the clearing. So every screen change
4541               	; 	' showed its counters and beams for several frames and then erased them,
4542               	; 	' which reads as the shop flickering rather than as it being drawn.
4543               	; 	'
4544               	; 	' TOP DOWN because a band's beam tops are stamped into the SLAB ROW OF
4545               	; 	' THE BAND ABOVE. Going bottom-up, band 1's blit would paint over the
4546               	; 	' beam tops band 0 had just put there. Going top-down the band above is
4547               	; 	' always already drawn, so everything a band needs -- its own cells and
4548               	; 	' the row it borrows overhead -- is final by the time its WAIT arrives.
4549               	; 	'
4550               	; 	' `3 - dq` rather than `FOR dlv = 3 TO 0 STEP -1`: dlv is an unsigned
4551               	; 	' byte, so counting down past zero wraps to 255 and the loop never ends
4552               	; 	' (CLAUDE.md 3A).
4553               	; draw_screen:
4554               cvb_DRAW_SCREEN
4555               	; 	GOSUB hide_play
4556 B908 06A0  32 	bl @jsr
     B90A A980     
4557 B90C EA36     	data cvb_HIDE_PLAY
4558               	; 	GOSUB load_band
4559 B90E 06A0  32 	bl @jsr
     B910 A980     
4560 B912 BCFC     	data cvb_LOAD_BAND
4561               	; 	FOR dq = 0 TO 3

4563 B916 D800  38 	movb r0,@cvb_DQ
     B918 21A0     
4564               cv127
4565               	; 		' WAIT FIRST, NOT LAST. A band is blitted and then corrected -- the
4566               	; 		' beam top stamped on, the pillar under a radio taken off -- and the
4567               	; 		' raster does not stop while that happens. Starting the burst in the
4568               	; 		' middle of a frame let the raster pass the band between the blit and
4569               	; 		' the correction, so the uncorrected version was shown for a frame:
4570               	; 		' at round start, after CLS, that reads as a support beam appearing
4571               	; 		' and being rubbed out. Beginning at vblank gives the whole burst a
4572               	; 		' clear run at the rows before the beam reaches them. It also still
4573               	; 		' gives each band its own frame, which is what stops a VDP write
4574               	; 		' burst past a few dozen from being silently dropped.
4575               	; 		WAIT

     B91C A980

4663               	;
4664               	; 	' A SUPPORT BEAM HAS TO REACH THE FLOOR IT HOLDS UP, AND THAT FLOOR IS
4665               	; 	' NOT IN ITS OWN BAND. A beam fills its band's four air rows, so its top
4666               	; 	' pixel sits under the slab row of the band ABOVE -- and a slab row is
4667               	; 	' five pixels of bar over three of green (see SLAB), so the beam stopped
4668               	; 	' three pixels short of the bar on every storey. It read as a beam that
4669               	; 	' misses the floor, which is exactly what it was.
4670               	; 	'
4671               	; 	' It cannot be drawn into either template. The beam belongs to the band
4672               	; 	' below and the row belongs to the band above, and the two templates are
4673               	; 	' chosen independently per screen, so no template knows both. So the
4674               	; 	' band blits go down first and the tops are stamped afterwards, from a
4675               	; 	' per-template column table -- the same shape as the escalator's head cap.
4676               	; 	'
4677               	; 	' Bands 0 and 1 only. Band 2's top row is the ROOF DECK, which is grey
4678               	; 	' over grey with no green to bridge, and the roof itself has no band
4679               	; 	' above it at all.
4680               	; 	' BANDS 0 TO 2 -- ALL THREE SHOPPING FLOORS. It used to stop at 1,
4681               	; 	' because band 2's ceiling is the ROOF DECK and the deck had no green
4682               	; 	' under it: a beam there already ran straight into grey and needed
4683               	; 	' nothing. Giving the roof its three green rows (so the lift shaft stops
4684               	; 	' under it, like every other floor) took that away, and the top floor's
4685               	; 	' beams started falling three pixels short.
4686               	; 	'
4687               	; 	' So the roof gets its own version of the stamp. A beam HOLDS THE ROOF
4688               	; 	' UP and must reach it; the shaft merely stops beneath it.
4689               	; beam_one:
4690               cvb_BEAM_ONE

5224               	; 			' AND HOW MANY FLOORS CARRY ONE AT ALL. The gates above
5225               	; 			' decide what KIND a hazard is; this decides whether the
5226               	; 			' floor has one.
5227               	; 			'
5228               	; 			' The kind-arrival rule downgrades anything that has not
5229               	; 			' arrived yet to a beach ball rather than leaving the floor
5230               	; 			' empty, so that a floor is never bare -- and the effect on
5231               	; 			' Krook 1 was a ball on all FOUR floors of every populated
5232               	; 			' screen. All four bands are on screen at once, so that is
5233               	; 			' four identical balls in view at all times, which is not
5234               	; 			' what "merely a few beach balls" describes.
5235               	; 			'
5236               	; 			' Per-band count was never the problem: only one slot is
5237               	; 			' live until Krook 6. It is the number of OCCUPIED FLOORS,
5238               	; 			' and it had no ramp at all.
5239               	; 			'
5240               	; 			' Floors 1 and 3 stay clear on Krook 1, floor 1 joins on
5241               	; 			' Krook 2, and the roof on Krook 3. ALTERNATING rather than
5242               	; 			' clearing the top or bottom half, so the empty floors do
5243               	; 			' not stack into a visibly dead region that reads as a bug
5244               	; 			' -- and the ROOF is last, because that is where the round
5245               	; 			' is decided.

5284               	; 			' NO SPRITE HAZARD ON THE FLOOR THE CROOK IS STANDING ON,
5285               	; 			' AND THE DECISION IS MADE HERE -- once, as the screen is
5286               	; 			' drawn, and never revisited while it is on screen.
5287               	; 			'
5288               	; 			' It was a test in the draw loop and another in the
5289               	; 			' collision test, both re-evaluated every frame, so the
5290               	; 			' hazards came and went as Harry walked on and off the
5291               	; 			' screen: run him to the edge and a floor's worth of balls
5292               	; 			' appeared out of nothing behind him. A rule about what
5293               	; 			' this screen CONTAINS cannot be a per-frame question --
5294               	; 			' the answer changes while the player is looking at it.
5295               	; 			'
5296               	; 			' Zeroing the kind here also makes both of those tests
5297               	; 			' unnecessary: an obstacle that was never loaded cannot be
5298               	; 			' drawn and cannot hit anybody.
5299               	; 			'
5300               	; 			' Radios are exempt. They are characters rather than
5301               	; 			' sprites, they do not move, and a fixture the crook runs

5380               	; 			' THEY COME FROM THE FAR SIDE. Kelly walked into this screen
5381               	; 			' from one edge, so the obstacles start at the OTHER edge and
5382               	; 			' run toward him. Crossing a seam then always presents an
5383               	; 			' ONCOMING stream, never a set of backs he has to catch up
5384               	; 			' with -- and it means the direction he is travelling is
5385               	; 			' always the direction the danger comes from.
5386               	; 			'
5387               	; 			' The table's x becomes a stagger from that edge rather than
5388               	; 			' an absolute position, and the slot index spreads them out
5389               	; 			' so they arrive in sequence instead of as one wall.
5390               	; 			'
5391               	; 			' THE SECOND ONE IS A FIXED DISTANCE BEHIND THE FIRST, AND
5392               	; 			' THAT DISTANCE IS SET BY THE JUMP.
5393               	; 			'
5394               	; 			' It used to be `(lx AND 63) + 64` -- a masked byte out of
5395               	; 			' the placement table plus a constant -- so the gap between
5396               	; 			' the two hazards was whatever those two bytes happened to
5397               	; 			' differ by. Measured across the five populated screens it
5398               	; 			' came out 46, 46, 70, 70, 70 px. Screens 2 and 4 flank the
5399               	; 			' elevator, so the store had a visibly tighter pair on one
5400               	; 			' side of it than the other, which is exactly how it was
5401               	; 			' reported from play.
5402               	; 			'
5403               	; 			' Both numbers are too small, which is the real fault. Kelly
5404               	; 			' closes on an oncoming hazard at WALKSP + the hazard's own
5405               	; 			' speed = 6 px a pass, and a jump lasts about 30 passes, so
5406               	; 			' a jump eats ~120 px of closing distance. At 46 or 70 there
5407               	; 			' is NO screen where he can land between the two: he clears
5408               	; 			' the first and comes down on the second.
5409               	; 			'
5410               	; 			' HAZGAP is that distance with margin. The first hazard
5411               	; 			' still lands where the table puts it, so the placement
5412               	; 			' variety is unchanged; only the pairing is now a fact about
5413               	; 			' the jump rather than an accident of two bytes.
5414               	; 			' assets/checkspace.py measures it and fails the build.
5415               	; 			' THE SECOND SLOT IS MEASURED FROM THE FIRST, not from its
5416               	; 			' own table byte. Adding HAZGAP to its own `lx AND 63` would
5417               	; 			' still leave the GAP varying by up to 63 px between screens
5418               	; 			' -- the same fault in a politer form, and the reason the two
5419               	; 			' sides of the elevator differed in the first place. Holding
5420               	; 			' slot 0's stagger and adding to that makes the gap exactly
5421               	; 			' HAZGAP on every screen, while slot 0 still lands wherever
5422               	; 			' the table puts it, so the placement variety is untouched.
5423               	; 			stag = lx AND 63

5503 BFF2 D401  30 	movb r1,*r0
5504               	; 			' THE BALL IS ON THE GROUND WHEN HE GETS TO IT.
5505               	; 			'
5506               	; 			' The bounce phase used to be `lx AND 31` -- a byte out of
5507               	; 			' the placement table, which is to say arbitrary. Run onto
5508               	; 			' a screen and the first ball might be at the bottom of its
5509               	; 			' arc, where a jump clears it, or at the top, where it has
5510               	; 			' to be ducked, and nothing on the way in told you which.
5511               	; 			' Sometimes you could keep running and jump it; sometimes
5512               	; 			' the same approach at the same speed could not be jumped
5513               	; 			' at all. That is not difficulty, it is a coin toss.
5514               	; 			'
5515               	; 			' So the phase is worked BACKWARDS from the meeting. He
5516               	; 			' closes 4 px a pass and the ball 2, so they meet in
5517               	; 			' (gap / 6) passes; the phase advances one a pass and wraps
5518               	; 			' at 32, so seeding it with the NEGATIVE of that lands it on
5519               	; 			' phase 0 -- the ground -- exactly as they arrive.
5520               	; 			'
5521               	; 			' The gap is the same either way round: he enters at one
5522               	; 			' wall and the ball starts `stag` in from the other, so it
5523               	; 			' is 232 - stag whichever direction the floor runs.
5524               	; 			'
5525               	; 			' It stays forgiving rather than exact. The arc is flat near
5526               	; 			' the bottom, so anything within eight passes either side is
5527               	; 			' still under the jump; and it only holds for an unbroken
5528               	; 			' run, which is the case the player is entitled to read off
5529               	; 			' the screen. Duck, stop or take a hit and everything after
5530               	; 			' is out of step again -- which is the game.
5531               	; 			obp(li) = lx AND 31
5532 BFF4 D020  34 	movb @cvb_LX,r0
     BFF6 21D9

     C73C C7DA     
6425               cv298
6426               	; 		' HE IS STANDING ON A STEP, so he moves at the STEP's rate: 2 px
6427               	; 		' across and 1 up PER PASS -- the staircase's own slope, and exactly
6428               	; 		' what one phase of esc_tick moves the pattern by.
6429               	; 		'
6430               	; 		' NOT `fdv`, WHICH IS THE WHOLE POINT. Everything else in this loop
6431               	; 		' is paced by the frame delta, and pacing him that way too made him
6432               	; 		' outrun the steps the moment a pass cost more than one frame: his
6433               	; 		' feet started planted on a tread and ended floating above it.
6434               	; 		' Pacing the ANIMATION by fdv instead is worse -- four phases cannot
6435               	; 		' be stepped by 2 or 3 without aliasing, and the staircase runs
6436               	; 		' backwards (see esc_tick). The only arrangement that cannot drift
6437               	; 		' is the one where both are driven by the same fixed step, so the
6438               	; 		' rider is clocked by the pass, like the steps he is standing on.
6439               	; 		'
6440               	; 		' THE FIRST FEW PASSES ARE THE STEP ON. Walking on, his feet are on
6441               	; 		' the floor and the bottom tread is 4 to 7 px above them, so he
6442               	; 		' climbs at 2 px a pass until he catches it (the tread is rising at
6443               	; 		' 1, so the gap closes at 1 a pass) and then rides with it. Jumping
6444               	; 		' on he lands ON a tread, so eson is 0 and this never runs.
6445               	; 		IF esy < eson THEN esy = esy + 1
6446 C73E D020  34 	movb @cvb_ESY,r0
     C740 2130     
6447 C742 9800  34 	cb r0,@cvb_ESON
     C744 20CD

     C74A C758

     C74E 2130

     C752 0100

     C756 2130

6747               	;
6748               	; 	' --- running
6749               	; 	' THE END WALLS ARE MOVEMENT LIMITS, NOT A CLAMP APPLIED AFTERWARDS.
6750               	; 	'
6751               	; 	' They used to be the latter, and that is the whole bug: on screen 7 Kelly
6752               	; 	' walked on to x = 254, the crossing routine then noticed there was no
6753               	; 	' screen 8 and set him to 232 -- a 22 px snap BACKWARDS. He reached the
6754               	; 	' wall and was thrown off it. Testing the limit BEFORE the step means he
6755               	; 	' arrives at the wall and stays there, which is what a wall does.
6756               	; 	' IN THE AIR HE IS BALLISTIC. The horizontal direction is fixed at
6757               	; 	' take-off and the stick is ignored until he lands: steering in mid-air
6758               	; 	' is not a jump, it is flight, and it also let him flip which way he was
6759               	; 	' facing halfway through an arc. kjdx is 0 for a standing jump, 1 for a
6760               	; 	' running one to the right, 2 to the left.

7076               	; 	' ------------------------------------------------------------ escalator
7077               	; 	' ONE working escalator per floor, at that floor's alternating end --
7078               	; 	' west, east, west. An escalator drawn where none works would be a step
7079               	; 	' the player runs to and nothing happens.
7080               	; 	' ESCALATORS RUN BOTH WAYS. Stand at the foot and you are carried up;
7081               	; 	' stand at the head of the flight that arrives from the floor below and
7082               	; 	' you are carried down. It is one staircase used from either end, not two
7083               	; 	' -- which is also why the down zone on a floor is the UP zone of the
7084               	; 	' floor beneath it, offset by one band.
7085               	; 	' UP ONLY. Riding down was built at one point and has been taken out
7086               	; 	' again at the reviewer's direction -- these only ever climb.
7087               	; 	'
7088               	; 	' Losing it also removed the re-board lock, which existed solely because
7089               	; 	' stepping off at the head put you inside the zone that rode the same
7090               	; 	' flight back down. With no down ride there is no such zone: a floor's
7091               	; 	' escalator is at the opposite end from the one below it, so arriving at
7092               	; 	' the top of one never lands you on the foot of another.

7651               	;
7652               	; 	' THE VICTROLA, AS CHARACTERS. It never moves, so a sprite spent a
7653               	; 	' scan-line slot on something that can be painted once when the screen is
7654               	; 	' drawn -- and at one 16x16 sprite it could only ever be one colour and
7655               	; 	' one size. Four cells is twice the area for no sprite at all.
7656               	; 	'
7657               	; 	' It stands ON the slab, so it fills the band's rows 2 and 3; row 4 is
7658               	; 	' the floor bar itself and draw_prizes uses row 3 for a collectible,
7659               	; 	' which is why the two never share a screen (genstore keeps prizes off
7660               	; 	' the screens that carry hazards).
7661               	; 	'
7662               	; 	' rdall = 1 paints all four cells, 0 repaints only the top-right one --
7663               	; 	' the pulse. Same address arithmetic either way, so the two cannot drift.
7664               	; 	' A BEAM TOP HANGS OUT OF THE FLOOR ABOVE, and the beam it belongs to has
7665               	; 	' just been taken away. `beam_tops` stamps SLABP -- the floor bar with the
7666               	; 	' support carried up through its lower three pixel rows -- into the slab
7667               	; 	' row above every pillar, and it runs BEFORE the radios and prizes are
7668               	; 	' placed. Clearing a pillar's own cells therefore left three grey pixels
7669               	; 	' dangling from the ceiling with nothing under them.
7670               	; 	'
7671               	; 	' TESTED, NOT ASSUMED: only SLABP is put back to SLAB. The row above the
7672               	; 	' top shopping floor is the ROOF DECK, a different character altogether,
7673               	; 	' and writing a floor bar over it would punch a hole in the roof.
7674               	; 	' A SHELF RUN IS FIVE CELLS WIDE and a radio covers two, so one standing
7675               	; 	' on a counter punched a hole through its middle and left the ends either
7676               	; 	' side -- the same incoherence the dangling pillar had, and worse, because
7677               	; 	' a counter reads as one object. The whole unit goes: walk outward along
7678               	; 	' the band's row 2 while the cell is still a shelf top, clearing it and
7679               	; 	' its bottom half together.
7680               	; 	'
7681               	; 	' Bounded at eight steps. The runs are five, so eight is slack rather
7682               	; 	' than a guess -- and an unbounded walk would run off the end of the row
7683               	; 	' if the name table ever held something unexpected.
7684               	; wipe_shelf:
7685               cvb_WIPE_SHELF

7983               	; 				' THE FIXTURE IT STANDS IN FRONT OF GOES AWAY.
7984               	; 				' A pillar fills its band's four air rows and the
7985               	; 				' radio covers only the lower two, so a radio
7986               	; 				' placed on one left the pillar's top half
7987               	; 				' hanging in the air above it -- a grey stub with
7988               	; 				' nothing under it. Shelves are covered outright
7989               	; 				' by the radio's own two rows, so only the rows

8354               	; 				' THEY WRAP. They do not turn round at the wall.
8355               	; 				' A bouncing obstacle is a pendulum: it has a near

8917               	;
8918               	; 	' THE SIXTEENTH-PIXEL ACCUMULATOR. hx is one unsigned byte per screen
8919               	; 	' (2), so there is nowhere to keep a fraction -- the fraction lives here
8920               	; 	' and is spent as whole pixels. THREE IFs, not a loop and not a divide:
8921               	; 	' `%` and `/` both compile to a real TMS9900 DIV (CLAUDE.md 3A) and this
8922               	; 	' runs every pass.
8923               	; 	'
8924               	; 	' THE NUMBER OF DRAINS IS A CEILING ON THE SPEED, AND THAT IS THE WHOLE
8925               	; 	' POINT OF THIS COMMENT. Each IF can spend one whole pixel, so N drains
8926               	; 	' cap hspd at N px per pass NO MATTER WHAT hsp64 SAYS. The invariant is:
8927               	; 	'
8928               	; 	'     hacc is under 16 on entry, and hsp64 is at most 48,
8929               	; 	'     so hacc is under 64 here and three drains always suffice.
8930               	; 	'
8931               	; 	' KEEP hsp64 <= 48. This was quarter-pixels with TWO drains and the same
8932               	; 	' invariant written the same way -- "hsp4 is at most 8, so it can never
8933               	; 	' need a third" -- and it was correct until somebody set hsp4 = 9 without
8934               	; 	' re-reading it. The crook then ran at a flat 2.0 px/pass while every
8935               	; 	' comment, the design table and assets/checkchase.py all said 2.25, and
8936               	; 	' the surplus quarter leaked into hacc and wrapped the byte every 256
8937               	; 	' passes. Nothing failed; he simply arrived two thirds of a screen short
8938               	; 	' of his own escape. checkchase.py now counts these IFs and refuses a
8939               	; 	' speed the drains cannot deliver.
8940               	; 	'
8941               	; 	' 7, NOT 6, AND THE REASON IS MEASURED. Tracked off the reference video
8942               	; 	' (DESIGN.md 4a), the 2600's Kelly covers 0.40 screen widths a second and
8943               	; 	' its Harry 0.193 -- a ratio of 2.07. At hsp4 = 6 ours was 2.67, and the
8944               	; 	' crook visibly strolled. 7 brings it to 2.29. 8 would match the original
8945               	; 	' exactly and cannot be used: Kelly's route here is 1.95x Harry's, so a
8946               	; 	' speed ratio of 2.0 leaves the on-foot chase 0.2 s of slack, which is no
8947               	; 	' chase at all. checkchase.py holds the line.
8948               	; 	' STILL RUNNING FROM THE STAIRS -- and this has to come BEFORE the move,
8949               	; 	' not after it. It sat below the two `IF hmv` blocks, where all it could
8950               	; 	' still reach was the animation counter: he faced the right way, played
8951               	; 	' the run cycle and did not travel a pixel. He stood at the foot of the
8952               	; 	' flight looking busy.
8953               	; 	'
8954               	; 	' TWO THINGS END THE RUN, AND BOTH ARE VISIBLE ON SCREEN.
8955               	; 	'
8956               	; 	' THE KOP ARRIVING ON THIS FLOOR OUTRANKS IT. This used to override the
8957               	; 	' flee as well as the climb, so for the whole of the run he held one
8958               	; 	' heading no matter where Kelly was -- and ran straight into him without
8959               	; 	' appearing to notice he was there. The flee above has already worked out
8960               	; 	' which way is away; clearing the run simply lets it stand.
8961               	; 	'
8962               	; 	' AND REACHING THE END WALL ENDS IT. It used to be a count of passes,
8963               	; 	' which expired somewhere in the middle of a floor and turned him round
8964               	; 	' for no reason the player could see. Running until the wall means every

9047               cv577
9048               	;
9049               	; 	' HE WALKS BY REAL TIME, NOT BY LOOP PASS -- one accumulate-and-drain per
9050               	; 	' ELAPSED FRAME rather than per pass. Everything that moves here used to be
9051               	; 	' per pass while the CLOCK is paced by the frame delta, so a busy screen
9052               	; 	' slowed Harry in real seconds and the clock did not slow with him: the
9053               	; 	' same uninterrupted escape finished with nine timer units in hand from a
9054               	; 	' light screen and none at all from the lift screen, decided by where the
9055               	; 	' player happened to be standing. Now he covers the same ground per second
9056               	; 	' wherever anybody is (DESIGN.md 0f-ter).
9057               	; 	'
9058               	; 	' A LOOP, NOT A MULTIPLY. `*` compiles to a real TMS9900 MPY, and reading a
9059               	; 	' 16-bit variable straight after one returns the product's HIGH word
9060               	; 	' (CLAUDE.md 3A). fdv is 1..3 here, so this is at most three adds.
9061               	; 	'
9062               	; 	' SIXTY-FOURTHS, AND ONLY TWO DRAINS -- both fall out of accumulating per
9063               	; 	' FRAME instead of per pass. He covers under a pixel a frame, so two drains
9064               	; 	' are ample where the per-pass version needed three; and the finer unit is
9065               	; 	' what makes the speed tunable at all. In sixteenths one step was about six
9066               	; 	' SECONDS of his route, so nothing landed near the buzzer; in sixty-fourths
9067               	; 	' it is about one and a half. Keep hsp64 <= 128: hacc is under 64 on entry,
9068               	; 	' so 64 + 128 stays inside a byte and two drains always suffice.
9069               	; 	'
9070               	; 	' AND THE STEP IS CLAMPED TO THREE FRAMES' WORTH, WHICH IS NOT A DETAIL.
9071               	; 	' Two tests sample his position once a pass and neither interpolates:
9072               	; 	'
9073               	; 	'   ARRIVAL   `hdx < 6` around the escalator or the roof door -- an ELEVEN
9074               	; 	'             pixel window. A step over 10 px can jump clean across it and
9075               	; 	'             he never arrives at all.
9076               	; 	'   THE CATCH `hdd < CATCHR` is 12, a 23-wide band, and Kelly closes 4 of
9077               	; 	'             it himself -- so Harry over ~18 px could pass through the Kop
9078               	; 	'             between two passes without either sample being inside it.
9079               	; 	'
9080               	; 	' FIVE FRAMES, AND THREE WAS TOO FEW. With two drains he moves at most 2 px
9081               	; 	' a frame, so five frames is 10 px -- and to skip the window he would have
9082               	; 	' to start outside it on one side and land outside it on the other, which
9083               	; 	' needs a step of 12. Ten is safe with a pixel to spare, and 10 + 4 = 14
9084               	; 	' against a 23-wide catch band.
9085               	; 	'
9086               	; 	' It was three, and three was the same bug in miniature. The lift screen
9087               	; 	' runs at almost exactly 20 passes a second, which IS fdv = 3, so every
9088               	; 	' momentary hitch there pushed past the clamp and he lost that frame --
9089               	; 	' measured as TIME 02 left on the lift screen against TIME 04 on a light
9090               	; 	' one, where before the real-time change it had been 00 against 09. The
9091               	; 	' clamp only exists for a genuine stall, and at five it only fires there.
9092               	; 	'
9093               	; 	' The ESCALATOR RIDE is deliberately untouched: it returns above this,
9094               	; 	' clocked one step per pass to stay locked to the moving staircase. A rider
9095               	; 	' advanced by the frame delta either drifts off the treads or makes the
9096               	; 	' steps run backwards (CLAUDE.md 3A, DESIGN.md 0f). It is about 3% of his
9097               	; 	' journey.
9098               	; 	'
9099               	; 	' `hanim` still advances by hspd and is still an ODOMETER -- pixels
9100               	; 	' travelled, not time -- so the run cycle stays locked to the ground at any
9101               	; 	' rate. A 9 px step moves it exactly one pose of its 8 px beat.

9321               	; 	' hspd is 0 on a third of his frames. On every one of those the legs took
9322               	; 	' another step while the ground did not move, which is precisely what
9323               	; 	' scrubbing your feet looks like. It is the classic foot-slip: the legs
9324               	; 	' and the world running off two different clocks.

9585 DE36 C03A  30 	mov *r10+,r0
9586 DE38 0450  20 	b *r0
9587               	;
9588               	; 	' ======================================================================
9589               	; 	' COLLISIONS -- pure arithmetic against RAM, no VDP reads
9590               	; 	' ======================================================================
9591               	; 	' Kelly's box is [kfh, kfh + kh) above the slab; an obstacle's is
9592               	; 	' [ohb, oht). The ball's inset hitbox is what makes 8..10 avoidable both
9593               	; 	' ways -- without it there is a height at which the ball can be neither
9594               	; 	' jumped nor ducked, and an unavoidable hazard is not difficulty.
9595               	; 	' ONE HIT PER CONTACT, WITH A REFRACTORY PERIOD.
9596               	; 	'
9597               	; 	' The overlap test is true on every frame the player is touching
9598               	; 	' something, so with no latch at all one clumsy cart charges nine seconds
9599               	; 	' several times over -- and what the player sees is the clock jumping by
9600               	; 	' 27, from which the only available conclusion is that the penalty is
9601               	; 	' broken.
9602               	; 	'
9603               	; 	' A latch that clears on the first non-overlapping frame is still not
9604               	; 	' enough, and the case that breaks it is the BOUNCING BALL: it rises off
9605               	; 	' the player between bounces, which is a genuine loss of contact, so a
9606               	; 	' player standing still under one is taxed on every single bounce while
9607               	; 	' being given no opportunity to do anything about it. Technically two
9608               	; 	' collisions; in play, one situation.
9609               	; 	'
9610               	; 	' So obht() is a countdown, not a flag. A hit sets it to HITREF; it ticks
9611               	; 	' down only while the player is CLEAR, and the same obstacle cannot charge
9612               	; 	' again until it reaches zero. Contact holds the count up, so parking
9613               	; 	' inside something never earns a second penalty either.
9614               	; coll_obst:
9615               cvb_COLL_OBST
9616               	; 	IF klst = ST_ESC THEN RETURN		' riding is invincible, per the
9617 DE3A D020  34 	movb @cvb_KLST,r0
     DE3C 219C     
9618 DE3E 0201  20 	li r1,768   ; 3*256
     DE40 0300

9913               	; 	' THE FLOOR CLEARS WHEN YOU ARE HIT, and stays clear until you re-enter
9914               	; 	' the screen. Nine seconds is a heavy penalty on a fifty-unit clock, and
9915               	; 	' taking it while still standing among the things that charged it -- with
9916               	; 	' a second hazard 48 px behind the first -- is how one mistake becomes
9917               	; 	' three. Clearing the row makes the penalty a single event you can walk
9918               	; 	' away from.
9919               	; 	'
9920               	; 	' BOTH SLOTS, WHATEVER IS IN THEM. Radios used to be exempt: they are
9921               	; 	' CHARACTERS stamped into the name table rather than sprites, so zeroing
9922               	; 	' the kind would have stopped them colliding while leaving them plainly
9923               	; 	' visible on the shelf, and a fixture that is still there has to still be
9924               	; 	' there. That was a reason to UNDRAW them, not a reason to keep them --
9925               	; 	' and it left the one hazard that cannot leave on its own as the only one
9926               	; 	' the mercy did not cover. haz_off now takes the characters with it.
9927               	; 	'
9928               	; 	' NOTHING RESTORES IT HERE. load_band repopulates the band from the
9929               	; 	' template, and it runs only on a seam crossing or a round start, so
9930               	; 	' "until the screen is re-entered" costs no state and no timer: leave and
9931               	; 	' come back and the hazards are simply placed again.
9932               	; 	GOSUB haz_gone
9933 E0BA 06A0  32 	bl @jsr
     E0BC A980

10102               	; 	' NO +8 ON EITHER SIDE, AND THAT IS THE FIX RATHER THAN A TIDY-UP. Both
10103               	; 	' centres are the left edge plus eight, so the eight cancels in the
10104               	; 	' difference -- but adding it first OVERFLOWED THE BYTE. Harry walks to
10105               	; 	' x 253 before he crosses a seam, and 253 + 8 wraps to 5, so a Kop
10106               	; 	' standing at the far LEFT of the screen measured five pixels to a crook
10107               	; 	' walking off the far RIGHT and arrested him across the whole store.
10108               	; 	'
10109               	; 	' Nothing warned: both are plain 8-bit variables, the wrap is silent, and
10110               	; 	' the catch it produces looks like a generous hitbox rather than like
10111               	; 	' arithmetic. The obstacle test has the same shape but cannot reach it --
10112               	; 	' obx tops out at 240 and klx at XWALL, so neither sum passes 255.

10147               	; 	' KELLY FLASHES WHILE HE IS KNOCKED ABOUT. Hitting an obstacle set a
10148               	; 	' 20-frame `knock` and took nine seconds off the clock, and NOTHING on
10149               	; 	' screen said so -- the time simply went. A flash is the arcade's own
10150               	; 	' idiom for "that hit you", costs one variable, and is readable even
10151               	; 	' when the collision happened off the edge of the player's attention.
10152               	; 	kcol = C_KHAT

10175               	; 	' KELLY IS SPRITES 0, 1 AND 2 and nothing else ever is -- the VDP drops
10176               	; 	' the highest-numbered sprites on an over-full scanline, so the lowest
10177               	; 	' slots are the ones that can never disappear, and the player is the one
10178               	; 	' thing that must never disappear.
10179               	; 	'
10180               	; 	' THE BAND SPRITES SHARE A y ON PURPOSE. Slots 0 and 1 both sit at ky and
10181               	; 	' between them cover rows 0-15; slot 2 sits at ky+16. Boxes 0/1 and box 2
10182               	; 	' never share a scanline, so an actor costs at most TWO boxes on any line
10183               	; 	' -- and the VDP counts boxes, not pixels, so an empty overlap would have
10184               	; 	' cost just as much as a full one. Two actors meeting is four, exactly
10185               	; 	' the per-line limit, which is why obstacles are suppressed on Harry's
10186               	; 	' floor rather than merely being a kindness.
10187               	; 	ky = flry(klv)

10331               	; 		' FOUR RUN FRAMES FROM TWO BITS of the animation counter, and no
10332               	; 		' divide -- `/` compiles to a real TMS9900 DIV (CLAUDE.md 3A) and
10333               	; 		' this is per-frame code. Two adjacent bits give 0,4,8,12.
10334               	; 		'
10335               	; 		' KELLY USES BITS 2 AND 3 AND HARRY USES 3 AND 4, because a stride
10336               	; 		' has to cover about a stride's worth of ground or the figure
10337               	; 		' skates. Kelly runs 4 px a frame, so four frames a pose is 16 px;
10338               	; 		' Harry runs 1.75, so eight frames a pose is 14. Matching the two
10339               	; 		' rates to the two speeds is what keeps both looking like running.

     E630 21AE     
10621               	; 	' HIS ARMS SWING, on the same bit of the animation counter that picks the
10622               	; 	' legs -- so the arm that is forward is the one opposite the leading leg,
10623               	; 	' which is what running looks like.
10624               	; 	' THE ARMS SWING ONCE PER FULL LEG CYCLE, ON BIT 16 -- not on bit 8.
10625               	; 	'
10626               	; 	' Bit 8 is one BEAT, and the legs take four of them (bits 8 and 16). So an
10627               	; 	' arm swing keyed to bit 8 completed twice for every one pass of the legs:
10628               	; 	' the top half of him flapping at double the speed of the bottom half.
10629               	; 	' That is the jig. It is not a drawing problem and no amount of redrawing
10630               	; 	' the legs would have touched it.
10631               	; 	'
10632               	; 	' On bit 16 the arms hold through beats 1-2 and swap for 3-4, which is
10633               	; 	' also the right PHASE: beat 1 leads with one leg and beat 3 with the
10634               	; 	' other, so the arm opposite the leading leg is forward in each -- which
10635               	; 	' is what running looks like.
10636               	; 	' THE TORSO RUNS ON THE SAME TWO BITS AS THE LEGS. With four drawn poses
10637               	; 	' the shoulders and arms swing WITH the stride; the old two-frame body had
10638               	; 	' a clock of its own, which is what read as a flap rather than a run.
10639               	; 	hp = P_HBODY

10715               	; 	' HE IS STRIPED FROM CAP TO HEM. Both stripe colours run the whole upper
10716               	; 	' half, so both boxes span rows 0-15 and neither can be tucked away --
10717               	; 	' affordable only because Kelly's hat moved into a box of its own. His
10718               	; 	' FACE is pushed DOWN instead (hy+3, box 3..18) so it clears the cap
10719               	; 	' rows, where his two stripe boxes plus Kelly's hat and face already
10720               	; 	' make four. Worst line of a meeting: exactly four, nothing dropped.
10721               	; 	' HE RISES A PIXEL ON THE RECOVERY BEATS. A runner is highest at mid-flight
10722               	; 	' and lowest at contact; with the body pinned at a constant y, alternating
10723               	; 	' legs under a motionless torso reads as a dance rather than as travel.
10724               	; 	'
10725               	; 	' One pixel is enough at this size, and it lifts the BODY ONLY -- the legs
10726               	; 	' keep their y, so the planted foot stays on the floor and the figure
10727               	; 	' stretches at the waist instead of hopping. Lifting the legs too would be
10728               	; 	' a jump.
10729               	; 	'
10730               	; 	' Beats 2 and 4 are the recoveries, which is exactly `hanim AND 8` -- the
10731               	; 	' cycle's LOW beat bit. IT MUST MOVE WITH THE BEAT BITS ABOVE IT: on the
10732               	; 	' wrong bit he rises and falls twice per stride, a second clock inside one
10733               	; 	' figure, which is the flapping of DESIGN.md 0j2 in the vertical.
10734               	; 	'
10735               	; 	' Safe because HARRY'S COLLISION HAS NO VERTICAL COMPONENT: he is caught on
10736               	; 	' `same level AND |dx| < CATCHR`. Doing this to Kelly would desynchronise
10737               	; 	' his sprite from the ball and biplane windows in DESIGN.md 5a, which is
10738               	; 	' why it is not done to him.
10739               	; 	' THE WHOLE FIGURE RISES, legs included, rather than the body stretching
10740               	; 	' away from the feet. Two reasons, and the second was not the first plan:
10741               	; 	'
10742               	; 	'   * It is what a run does. Mid-flight BOTH feet are off the ground, so
10743               	; 	'     lifting the legs with the body is the correct pose, not a compromise.
10744               	; 	'   * Lifting the body alone opens a one-pixel gap at the waist, and the
10745               	; 	'     torso's bottom row and the legs' top row do not align across it --
10746               	; 	'     green shows through and he reads as coming apart.
10747               	; 	'
10748               	; 	' It also keeps `hfy = hy - 7` and `hy2 = hy + 16` LITERAL, which is not a
10749               	; 	' cosmetic concern: assets/checkbands.py reads each band's draw offset by
10750               	; 	' parsing those exact expressions. Bobbing a differently-named variable
10751               	; 	' made them unreadable and the gate failed the build -- correctly. A
10752               	; 	' checker that can no longer see what it checks has to fail, not shrug.
10753               	; 	IF hanim AND 8 THEN hy = hy - 1

10942               	; 		' THE SCREEN TEST IS WHAT MAKES IT INVISIBLE. Clearing his whole
10943               	; 		' floor regardless of screen meant the cleared band moved with him,
10944               	; 		' so every time he changed storeys one floor's hazards vanished and
10945               	; 		' another's appeared in the same instant -- and with all four bands
10946               	; 		' in view at once that reads as objects hopping between levels. Now
10947               	; 		' it only happens on the screen he is actually on, where the reason
10948               	; 		' for it is standing right there.
10949               	; 		'
10950               	; 		' Radios are unaffected: they are characters, drawn straight into
10951               	; 		' the name table, and a fixture the crook happens to run past is

11220 EABA C03A  30 	mov *r10+,r0
11221 EABC 0450  20 	b *r0
11222               	;
11223               	; 	' ======================================================================
11224               	; 	' THE SCANNER
11225               	; 	' ======================================================================
11226               	; 	' Rows 21-23, 16 chars wide, centred: 48 characters whose PATTERNS are
11227               	; 	' poked directly, so a moving dot costs two writes and no name-table
11228               	; 	' traffic. With the flip (DESIGN.md 2a) this is how you know where Harry
11229               	; 	' is at all -- he can be one pixel off-screen and completely invisible.
11230               	; 	'
11231               	; 	' Rows 21-23 all sit in the THIRD screen third, so every scanner pattern
11232               	; 	' is at base 4096 and no third-selection arithmetic is needed.
11233               	; 	'
11234               	; 	' BOTH DOTS ARE WHITE and Kelly's BLINKS. The manual wants Kelly black
11235               	; 	' and Harry white, but this is the TMS9918's two-colours-per-8x1-cell
11236               	; 	' mode: a black dot and a white dot on the same pixel row of the same
11237               	; 	' character cannot both exist. Blinking distinguishes them with one
11238               	; 	' colour, which is what RallyX does with its player dot.
11239               	; 	' THE STEPS MOVE, AND EVERY ESCALATOR ON SCREEN MOVES WITH THEM. A flight
11240               	; 	' is twelve characters -- six per direction, sliced out of a rendered
11241               	; 	' 64x40 bitmap by genart.py -- so rewriting those twelve PATTERNS animates
11242               	; 	' the lot: 96 bytes, no name-table traffic, and the same cost whether one
11243               	; 	' flight is visible or ten. The phases slide the cells ALONG the slope, so
11244               	; 	' the steps travel up the flight rather than drifting sideways.
11245               	; esc_tick:
11246               cvb_ESC_TICK
11247               	; 	' ONLY SCREENS 0 AND 7 CARRY A FLIGHT, so six screens in eight skip this
11248               	; 	' entirely -- which is what makes rewriting the cells every frame
11249               	; 	' affordable. Nested, not `klsc > 0 AND klsc < 7`: the 9900 backend
11250               	; 	' miscompiles a compare-AND-compare (CLAUDE.md 3A).
11251               	; 	IF klsc > 0 THEN
11252 EABE D020  34 	movb @cvb_KLSC,r0
     EAC0 218A     
11253 EAC2 0201  20 	li r1,256   ; 1*256
     EAC4 0100     
11254 EAC6 9040  18 	cb r0,r1
11255 EAC8 1402  14 	jhe cv800
11256 EACA 0460  28 	b @cv799
     EACC EAE2     
11257               cv800
11258               	; 		IF klsc < 7 THEN RETURN
11259 EACE D020  34 	movb @cvb_KLSC,r0
     EAD0 218A     
11260 EAD2 0201  20 	li r1,1792   ; 7*256
     EAD4 0700     
11261 EAD6 9040  18 	cb r0,r1
11262 EAD8 1A02  14 	jl cv802
11263 EADA 0460  28 	b @cv801
     EADC EAE2     
11264               cv802
11265 EADE C03A  30 	mov *r10+,r0
11266 EAE0 0450  20 	b *r0
11267               cv801
11268               	; 	END IF
11269               cv799
11270               	; 	' ONE PHASE PER PASS, AND NEVER MORE. There are four phases covering an
11271               	; 	' 8 px period, so advancing by the frame delta ALIASES: at fdv 2 the
11272               	; 	' pattern flips between two positions half a period apart and the
11273               	; 	' direction stops being readable, and at fdv 3 the sequence runs 0,3,2,1
11274               	; 	' -- the staircase visibly runs BACKWARDS, carrying its steps down while
11275               	; 	' the rider goes up. That is the wagon-wheel effect, and no amount of
11276               	; 	' phase arithmetic fixes it: a 4-phase cycle can only be stepped by 1.
11277               	; 	'
11278               	; 	' So the ANIMATION is the clock here, and the rider is paced from it --
11279               	; 	' see move_kelly, which advances him by the same fixed 2-and-1 per pass
11280               	; 	' rather than by fdv. Locking the two together is the only way they
11281               	; 	' cannot drift, and it is what "the player moves at the rate the steps
11282               	; 	' carry him" actually means. The cost is that a ride takes 36 passes
11283               	; 	' rather than 36 frames, so it slows down when the loop does -- which is
11284               	; 	' the honest behaviour for a machine that is carrying you.
11285               	; 	escp = escp + 1
11286 EAE2 D020  34 	movb @cvb_ESCP,r0
     EAE4 20B1     
11287 EAE6 0220  22 	ai r0,256	; 1
     EAE8 0100     
11288 EAEA D800  38 	movb r0,@cvb_ESCP
     EAEC 20B1

     EAF0 0400

     EAF8 EB00

     EAFE 20B1     
11297               cv803
11298               	; 	' ONLY THE CELLS THAT MOVE, AND ONLY THIS SCREEN'S. genart.py measures
11299               	; 	' which cells move -- the step cells and the COMPOSITE copies of them
11300               	; 	' that cross a floor -- and groups them WEST then EAST, because only one
11301               	; 	' direction is ever on screen: screen 0 carries a west flight and screen
11302               	; 	' 7 an east one.
11303               	; 	'
11304               	; 	' THIS IS THE ONLY PER-PASS WORK UNIQUE TO THESE TWO SCREENS, and it is
11305               	; 	' `define_char`, which in bitmap mode is LDIRVM3 -- the TRIPLE copy, once
11306               	; 	' per screen third. It therefore costs three times what the character
11307               	; 	' count suggests, which is why these screens run slower than the rest.
11308               	; 	'
11309               	; 	' THE WEST SCREEN USED TO COST HALF AS MUCH AGAIN, because it carries TWO
11310               	; 	' flights: floor 1's crosses into a shop floor and floor 3's into the
11311               	; 	' roof, so it needed both composite sets -- nine characters against the
11312               	; 	' east's six, 216 bytes a pass against 144. It measured 20 passes a
11313               	; 	' second where the east screen managed 24-26. The two sets are
11314               	; 	' PIXEL-IDENTICAL and are never wanted in the same screen THIRD, so they
11315               	; 	' share their characters now and are told apart by the per-third colour
11316               	; 	' table (see esc_deck_col in setup). Six either side.
11317               	; 	'
11318               	; 	' The handrail and the frame sit past the end of both ranges and never
11319               	; 	' move at all.

11577 ED3A ED40     	data cvb_SCAN_FURN
11578               	; 	RETURN
11579 ED3C C03A  30 	mov *r10+,r0
11580 ED3E 0450  20 	b *r0
11581               	;
11582               	; 	' ------------------------------------------------- the scanner's furniture
11583               	; 	' Without this the scanner is two dots in a void: you can see THAT Harry is
11584               	; 	' somewhere, but not what he is near, which is the one thing you actually
11585               	; 	' need when he is off-screen. Floor lines say which level, the slashes say
11586               	; 	' where that floor's escalator is, and the bar says where the elevator is --
11587               	; 	' exactly the three things the manual lists.
11588               	; 	'
11589               	; 	' FURNITURE AND DOTS ARE SEGREGATED BY PIXEL ROW ON PURPOSE, and a level
11590               	; 	' band is now FOUR rows, one per colour, because a pixel row carries
11591               	; 	' exactly one colour here:
11592               	; 	'
11593               	; 	'   +0  GREY    escalator head, and the elevator car
11594               	; 	'   +1  GREY    the air of that floor
11595               	; 	'   +2  GREY
11596               	; 	'   +3  YELLOW  the floor line -- and NOTHING else goes here
11597               	; 	'
11598               	; 	' THE FIRST THREE ROWS ARE THE FLOOR'S AIR AND EVERYTHING LIVES IN THEM:
11599               	; 	' the escalator's three-step diagonal, the lift car, and both actors, all
11600               	; 	' three pixels tall. They are grey in the table, and the Kop and the
11601               	; 	' crook recolour their own CHARACTER as they move (scan_mark) -- which is
11602               	; 	' what lets both of them fill the band instead of getting a pixel row
11603               	; 	' each. Eight pixels of this canvas is half a screen of the store, so
11604               	; 	' they are almost never in one character; when they are, they merge.
11605               	; 	'
11606               	; 	' Shrinking from six rows bought eight empty pixel rows, four above the
11607               	; 	' instrument and four below, so it no longer butts against the shop floor
11608               	; 	' above it or the bottom of the screen.
11609               	; 	'
11610               	; 	' The dots are erased by ANDing their bits out, so anything sharing a row
11611               	; 	' with one is rubbed away wherever an actor has passed -- which is why
11612               	; 	' scan_escs and scan_elev redraw every tick rather than once.
11613               	; scan_furn:
11614               cvb_SCAN_FURN

11886               	;
11887               	; 	' THE FLIGHT IS BLACK, and it has to be written per character because the
11888               	; 	' colour table only knows rows -- the same reason the two actors carry

11891               	; 	' This runs BEFORE the markers every tick, which is the whole ordering
11892               	; 	' the radar needs: the lift car is painted first and the flights next, so
11893               	; 	' a Kop or a crook standing on either of them paints over it. Nothing
11894               	; 	' moves aside to make room -- the pixels are simply ORed together and the
11895               	; 	' last colour written wins, so an actor in the lift shaft shows as an
11896               	; 	' actor rather than shifting anywhere.

12030 F092 C03A  30 	mov *r10+,r0

12032               	;
12033               	; 	' Blank the canvas by WRITING zeros rather than copying 384 of them out
12034               	; 	' of ROM. This runs twice a game -- at setup and on a new Krook -- so a
12035               	; 	' loop costs nothing anybody can perceive, and it hands the fixed area
12036               	; 	' back the best part of half a kilobyte, which is the budget that
12037               	; 	' actually binds. Paced in bursts: a few hundred VDP writes in a single
12038               	; 	' frame are silently dropped (CLAUDE.md 3A).
12039               	; scan_wipe:
12040               cvb_SCAN_WIPE
12041               	; 	' THIS ADDRESS IS A CHARACTER CODE IN DISGUISE, AND IT WENT STALE.
12042               	; 	' 4096 is the pattern table's BOTTOM THIRD -- rows 16-23, which is
12043               	; 	' band 0 and the radar together -- and the canvas characters begin at
12044               	; 	' genart's SCAN_FIRST, so the base is 4096 + SCAN_FIRST*8. It was
12045               	; 	' written out by hand as 4096 + 160*8 when SCAN_FIRST was 160; art
12046               	; 	' added later moved SCAN_FIRST to 208 and nothing moved this with it,
12047               	; 	' so the wipe zeroed the 48 characters BELOW the canvas and never
12048               	; 	' touched the canvas at all.
12049               	; 	'
12050               	; 	' Two of those forty-eight are STRUCTURE. ENDWALL is the building's
12051               	; 	' outside wall and SLABP is a pillar's cap. Blanking a pattern in ONE
12052               	; 	' THIRD leaves a character that draws correctly everywhere else and
12053               	; 	' falls back to its own background colour here -- so the GROUND FLOOR's
12054               	; 	' outside wall turned into plain green while the identical wall one
12055               	; 	' storey up was perfect, and the ground floor's pillars lost the brick
12056               	; 	' carried up through the bar above them. ENDWALL appears only on
12057               	; 	' screens 0 and 7, which is exactly why it was reported as happening
12058               	; 	' only on the escalator screens, mirrored side to side.
12059               	; 	'
12060               	; 	' The name table was correct the whole time. Probing it said so, four
12061               	; 	' times. It was the GLYPH that was empty, in one third only -- which a
12062               	; 	' probe that copies the character somewhere else to look at it cannot
12063               	; 	' see, because the copy renders from a different third's pattern.
12064               	; 	'
12065               	; 	' renumber.py rewrites this line from genart.SCAN_FIRST, and
12066               	; 	' checkstruct.py fails the build if the two disagree.
12067               	; 	#swa = 5760			' 4096 + SCAN_FIRST*8, the canvas patterns
12068 F096 0200  20 	li r0,5760
     F098 1680

     F09C 20E0

12138               	;
12139               	; 	' THE TWO ACTORS ARE SPRITES; EVERYTHING ELSE IS CHARACTERS.
12140               	; 	'
12141               	; 	' That split is what makes the instrument simple. The floor lines, the
12142               	; 	' flights and the lift car do not move relative to the canvas, so they
12143               	; 	' are pixels in the scanner's own characters and cost nothing to keep.
12144               	; 	' The Kop and the crook DO move, and as sprites they need no colour
12145               	; 	' table, no erase and no priority arithmetic -- they draw over the
12146               	; 	' furniture because sprites always do, and nothing shifts to make room.
12147               	; 	'
12148               	; 	' IT ALSO REMOVED THE BLINK. Kelly flashed because a pixel row of this
12149               	; 	' canvas can only carry one colour, so at close range the two markers
12150               	; 	' merged and the blink was the only thing left to tell them apart. Two
12151               	; 	' sprites are two colours wherever they stand.
12152               	; 	'
12153               	; 	' The furniture is drawn ONCE, by scan_furn. It used to be redrawn every
12154               	; 	' tick because erasing a character-based marker ANDed bits out of
12155               	; 	' whatever it was standing on; there is nothing left to rub it away. The
12156               	; 	' car still redraws itself, because the car actually moves.
12157               	; 	' -- the lift car. IT WAS FLICKERING, and the cause was not the drawing
12158               	; 	' but the ERASING: scan_elev cleared its three cells and put them back
12159               	; 	' every tick, whether or not the car had moved, so several times a second
12160               	; 	' there was a window with the pixels off. A sprite has no erase at all --
12161               	; 	' it is placed, and the VDP does the rest -- and slot 26 sits BELOW the
12162               	; 	' two markers, so the Kop and the crook pass over it without either of
12163               	; 	' them having to know the car exists.

12641               	; 	' ======================================================================
12642               	; 	' THE CLOCK
12643               	; 	' ======================================================================

12876               	;
12877               	; 	' THE BONUS IS COUNTED OUT, NOT AWARDED. Dropping the whole sum into the
12878               	; 	' score in one frame tells the player a number; ticking the clock down a
12879               	; 	' unit at a time, with the score climbing and a beat under each step,
12880               	; 	' tells them what the number was FOR. It is the pay-off for every second
12881               	; 	' they saved, and every arcade game of this era spends a moment on it.
12882               	; 	'
12883               	; 	' It also costs nothing to get right: the loop already has to run down
12884               	; 	' `tsec`, so the animation IS the calculation, and the repeated-addition
12885               	; 	' note below stops applying -- each step adds one band's worth.
12886               	; 	'
12887               	; 	' x100 for Krooks 1-9, x200 for 10-15, x300 from 16. Two independent
12888               	; 	' sources agree on those bands; the manual's own wording suggests
12889               	; 	' 1-8 / 9-16 / 17+ and DESIGN.md 0 records the disagreement.
12890               	; 	'
12891               	; 	' #bval MUST BE 16-BIT: 300 does not fit in a byte, and a plain variable
12892               	; 	' would silently truncate it to 44 (CLAUDE.md 3A).
12893               	; bonus_count:

12995 F7E4 0460  28 	b @cvb_BN_LOOP
     F7E6 F738     
12996               	;
12997               	; 	' `calc_bonus` USED TO LIVE HERE and computed the whole sum by repeated
12998               	; 	' addition, to dodge two hazards at once: `tsec * bmul` is an 8-bit
12999               	; 	' product (a 50-unit capture in the x300 band pays 15,000, six times past
13000               	; 	' a byte), and multiplying properly lands on the MPY/r0 hazard where a
13001               	; 	' 16-bit variable read straight after being multiplied returns the
13002               	; 	' product's high word. Counting the bonus out a unit at a time (above)
13003               	; 	' removes the multiply entirely rather than working around it -- the loop
13004               	; 	' the player watches IS the arithmetic.
13005               	; 	'
13006               	; 	' It also fixed the last-tick case for free: `bn_loop` tests `tsec = 0`
13007               	; 	' on entry, where the old `FOR bi = 1 TO tsec` would have run its body
13008               	; 	' once and paid a full multiplier for no time at all (CLAUDE.md 3A).
13009               	;
13010               	; do_escape:
13011               cvb_DO_ESCAPE
13012               	; 	escapd = 0
13013 F7E8 04C0  14 	clr r0
13014 F7EA D800  38 	movb r0,@cvb_ESCAPD
     F7EC 210A     
13015               	; 	rsn = 0
13016 F7EE 04C0  14 	clr r0
13017 F7F0 D800  38 	movb r0,@cvb_RSN
     F7F2 21F8

13050               	; 	' THE MESSAGE BOX, ON THE GAME SCREEN AND NOT INSTEAD OF IT.
13051               	; 	'
13052               	; 	' It used to CLS before GAME OVER, so the last thing the player saw was
13053               	; 	' the store being deleted and then two words on an empty field -- which
13054               	; 	' reads as the program ending rather than as the game ending. The store
13055               	; 	' stays; the message sits in the middle of it.
13056               	; 	'
13057               	; 	' THE BOX IS THE FONT'S OWN BACKGROUND. Every font character is black on
13058               	; 	' HUD_BG (dark blue), so a row of SPACES is a solid dark blue bar. A blank
13059               	; 	' row above and below the text is a frame for the price of two strings and
13060               	; 	' no new characters, and it reads against the store because nothing else on
13061               	; 	' the playfield is that colour.
13062               	; 	'
13063               	; 	' THIRTEEN WIDE, WHICH IS THE LONGEST MESSAGE PLUS ONE EITHER SIDE.
13064               	; 	' HE GOT AWAY and THE BIPLANE are both eleven. Every string below is padded
13065               	; 	' to exactly thirteen so the box has straight edges, and column 10 puts it
13066               	; 	' at columns 10-22 -- centre 16, the screen's.
13067               	; 	'
13068               	; 	' THE REASON IS ALWAYS ON THE SAME ROW, and that is what lets one layout
13069               	; 	' serve both cases. Losing a life draws three rows around it; losing the
13070               	; 	' last one draws two more ON TOP, so GAME OVER appears above the reason
13071               	; 	' rather than replacing it. Printing the reason at a different row for each

13237               	;
13238               	; 	' Silence every channel FIRST. SOUND latches, and the per-channel decay
13239               	; 	' counters are not ticked inside this loop -- a tone still sounding when
13240               	; 	' the pause starts would hang for its whole duration, which is the classic
13241               	; 	' CVBasic sticky-audio failure.
13242               	; 	' EVERY CHANNEL OFF, AND THE DECAY COUNTERS WITH THEM. The counters are
13243               	; 	' ticked by the main loop, so anything still sounding when the loop stops
13244               	; 	' -- for a capture, a death, the bonus count -- sustains for as long as
13245               	; 	' whatever replaced the loop takes. That is where the long beep over the
13246               	; 	' bonus tally came from: not the tally's own note, which turns itself off
13247               	; 	' after every unit, but a footstep or a hit that was still ringing when
13248               	; 	' Harry was caught.

13702 FD96 C03A  30 	mov *r10+,r0
13703 FD98 0450  20 	b *r0
13704               	;
13705               	; 	' EVERYTHING BELOW THIS LINE IS ASSEMBLED INTO BANK 1, so the INCLUDE order
13706               	; 	' is load-bearing and nothing but data may follow it.
13707               	; 	'
13708               	; 	' THE FONT IS NOW IN THE BANK TOO, and it used to be deliberately outside:
13709               	; 	' keeping one readable thing in bank 0 means a bank-selection mistake shows
13710               	; 	' as "text survives, art does not" rather than a uniformly blank screen,
13711               	; 	' which is a genuinely useful thing to have when a bank goes wrong.
13712               	; 	'
13713               	; 	' It was given up for 472 bytes, because the fixed area had 342 left and
13714               	; 	' that diagnostic is worth less than the ability to keep building. Two
13715               	; 	' things make it a fair trade rather than a straight loss:
13716               	; 	'
13717               	; 	'   * `BANK SELECT 1` already runs BEFORE `DEFINE CHAR 32,59,font_bits`
13718               	; 	'     (see setup), so nothing about the read order had to change. If that
13719               	; 	'     ever stops being true the font goes blank at boot, immediately and
13720               	; 	'     unmistakably -- a loud failure, not a subtle one.
13721               	; 	'   * The alternative on the table was banking the 29 PRINT AT literals,
13722               	; 	'     which recovers LESS (about 360 bytes net after the reader routine)
13723               	; 	'     and would blind assets/checklayout.py, whose whole method is parsing
13724               	; 	'     `PRINT AT n,"literal"` to catch a string running past column 31 or
13725               	; 	'     a HUD poke landing inside a label. Trading a diagnostic for bytes is
13726               	; 	'     one thing; trading a build gate for fewer bytes is another.
13727               	; 	#if TI994A
13728               	; 	BANK 1
13729      0264     BANK_0_FREE:	EQU >fffe-$
     **** ****     > .rept
